=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
We call a positive integer n a good integer if and only if the sum of its positive divisors is divisible by 3.
You are given two positive integers N and M. Find the number, modulo 998244353, of length-M sequences A of positive integers such that the product of the elements in A is a good integer not exceeding N.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 10^{10}
- 1 \leq M \leq 10^5
- N and M are integers.

Sample Input 1

10 1

Sample Output 1

5

There are five sequences that satisfy the conditions:

- (2)
- (5)
- (6)
- (8)
- (10)

Sample Input 2

4 2

Sample Output 2

2

There are two sequences that satisfy the conditions:

- (1, 2)
- (2, 1)

Sample Input 3

370 907

Sample Output 3

221764640

Sample Input 4

10000000000 100000

Sample Output 4

447456146

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

If p ≡ 0 mod3, i.e. p=3. σ(3^a)=1+3+...+3^a ≡1 mod3. Never divisible by3. So factor 3^a doesn't help, but can be present.

If p ≡ 2 mod3: powers alternate 1,2,1,2... σ(p^a)=1 if a even? Sum a+1 terms starting 1 +2 +1... If a even (odd number terms): sum ≡1 mod3? e.g a=0:1, a=2:1+2+1=4≡1. If a odd (even terms): 1+2=0 mod3. So divisible by3 iff a odd.

- p=3: never contributes.

Thus F(s) = 1/(1-3^{-s}) * ∏_{p≡1} (1+p^{-s})/(1-p^{-3s}) * ∏_{p≡2} 1/(1-p^{-2s}).

This is a product of Dirichlet series:
- 1/(1-3^{-s}) = ∑_{a≥0} 3^{-as} (powers of 3)
- ∏_{p≡1} (1+p^{-s}) = Dirichlet series of squarefree numbers with primes ≡1 mod3 (z)
- ∏_{p≡1} 1/(1-p^{-3s}) = Dirichlet series of cubes of numbers with primes ≡1 mod3 (w^3)
- ∏_{p≡2} 1/(1-p^{-2s}) = Dirichlet series of squares of numbers with primes ≡2 mod3 (y^2)
And since these local factors multiply, the Dirichlet series F(s) corresponds to the unique representation n=3^a z w^3 y^2. This is a Dirichlet convolution of the indicator functions of these sets? Actually the standard Dirichlet hyperbola for D_s = D_{s-1} * 1:

Standard hyperbola formula for convolution f = g * h:
S_f(n) = ∑_{i≤√n} g(i) S_h(⌊n/i⌋) + ∑_{j≤√n} h(j) S_g(⌊n/j⌋) - S_g(√n)S_h(√n).
For D_s = τ_s, with τ_s = τ_{s-1} * 1. Let g=τ_{s-1}, h=1. Then:
D_s(n) = ∑_{i≤√n} τ_{s-1}(i) * floor(n/i) + ∑_{j≤√n} D_{s-1}(⌊n/j⌋) - D_{s-1}(√n)*floor(√n).
This is exact and clean. Let's verify with small n,s. For s=2, τ_1=1, D_1(x)=x.
D_2(n)=∑_{i≤√n} floor(n/i) + ∑_{j≤√n} floor(n/j) - √n*√n = 2∑_{i≤√n} floor(n/i) - (floor√n)^2. This is the standard hyperbola for divisor summatory. Good.

So we can compute D_s(n) recursively using this formula. It requires:
- τ_{s-1}(i) for i≤√n (precomputed up to √N=1e5).
- D_{s-1}(⌊n/j⌋) for j≤√n (recursive calls).
- D_{s-1}(√n) (small, precomputed if √n≤L).

This recurrence might be efficient if we memoize D_s(x) for x values. Let's analyze complexity more carefully with this formula.

For D_s(N), the second sum has √N terms (j=1..√N). For each j, call D_{s-1}(floor(N/j)). These are large values. To compute D_{s-1}(X), we again use formula with √X terms. But with memoization, many calls share? The arguments floor(N/j) for j≤√N are distinct large values (about 1e5). For each, computing D_{s-1}(X) requires summing over j'≤√X, generating arguments floor(X/j') = floor(N/(j*j'))? These are also floor(N/t) for t up to N? def dfs(n, idx):
    # sum of f(m) for m <= n, m has no prime factor < primes[idx]? Actually returns sum over integers m≤n whose smallest prime factor ≥ primes[idx] (including 1?) Let's recall.
Min_25 formula:
S(n, y) = G(n) - G(p_{y-1}) + ∑_{i≥y, p_i^2≤n} ∑_{e≥1} [ f(p_i^e) * S(n / p_i^e, i+1) + f(p_i^{e+1}) ]
where G(x)=∑_{p≤x} f(p), and S(n,y) is sum of f(m) for m≤n with all prime factors ≥ p_y? Including m=1? Let's verify.

The total S_f(n) = S(n, 0) where p_0=1? Standard:
ll S(n, y) { // sum f(x) for x<=n, x's smallest prime factor >= p_y
  ans = G(n) - sum_{p<p_y} f(p);
  for i=y; i<primes.size() && p_i^2<=n; i++:
    pe = p_i;
    for e=1; pe*p_i<=n; e++, pe*=p_i:
       ans += f(pe) * S(n/pe, i+1) + f(pe*p_i);
  return ans;
}
This returns sum over numbers whose prime factors ≥ p_y, including 1? The G(n)-prefix accounts for primes ≥p_y. The loop adds composites with smallest prime factor p_i. The term f(pe)*S(n/pe, i+1) accounts for numbers with at least one more prime factor ≥p_i (since S includes 1 and primes/composites with factors ≥p_{i+1}); the +f(pe*p_i) accounts for powers p_i^{e+1}. This is correct.

Pseudo:

def dfs(n, idx):
    ansD = G_D[n] - prefD[idx]
    ansE = G_E[n] - prefE[idx]
    for i in range(idx, len(primes)):
        p = primes[i]
        if p*p > n: break
        pe = p
        e = 1
        while pe * p <= n:
            # pe = p^e
            subD, subE = dfs(n // pe, i+1)
            fD_e = comb[e]
            fE_e = fE(p,e)
            ansD += fD_e * subD + comb[e+1]  # f_D(p^{e+1})
            ansE += fE_e * subE + fE(p,e+1)
            pe *= p
            e += 1
    return ansD % MOD, ansE % MOD

The +f(p^{e+1}) counts pure powers with exponent e+1. This covers all.

Need ensure fE(p,e+1) computed. For p≡2, fE(p,1)=0, fE(p,2)=comb[2], etc.

Let's code first DP:

def compute_prime_sums(N):
    sqrtN = isqrt(N)
    primes = sieve(sqrtN)
    vals = []
    i=1
    while i<=N:
        v=N//i
        vals.append(v)
        i=N//v+1
    # vals decreasing
    m=len(vals)
    idx_map = {v:i for i,v in enumerate(vals)}
    h1 = [v-1 for v in vals]
    hChi = [(1 if v%3==1 else 0) - 1 for v in vals]
    # precompute pm_idx for primes? Need for each p in loop.
    # limits: for each prime, number of vals >= p^2.
    limit = m
    # We'll loop primes and update limit:
    for p in primes:
        p2 = p*p
        while limit > 0 and vals[limit-1] < p2:
            limit -= 1
        if limit == 0: break
        pm_idx = idx_map[p-1]
        sp = 1 if p%3==1 else (-1 if p%3==2 else 0)
        # initial ptr for vals[0]//p
        w = vals[0] // p
        ptr = idx_map[w]
        if sp == 0:
            for j in range(limit):
                v = vals[j]
                w = v // p
                while ptr < m and vals[ptr] > w:
                    ptr += 1
                h1[j] -= h1[ptr] - h1[pm_idx]
        else:
            for j in range(limit):
                v = vals[j]
                w = v // p
                while ptr < m and vals[ptr] > w:
                    ptr += 1
                h1[j] -= h1[ptr] - h1[pm_idx]
                hChi[j] -= sp * (hChi[ptr] - hChi[pm_idx])
    return vals, idx_map, h1, hChi, primes

Now, fE(p,e) function:
def fE(p,e):
    if p == 3:
        return comb[e]
    r = p % 3
    if r == 1:
        if e % 3 == 2: return 0
        return comb[e]
    else: # r==2
        if e & 1: return 0
        return comb[e]
Since primes list only up to sqrtN, p=2,3,5,... p%3 never 0 except 3.

In DFS, we need fE(p,e+1) for e+1 up to MAXE. We can compute on the fly.

MAXE: max exponent such that 2^e ≤ N? Actually p^{e+1}≤n≤N, so e+1 ≤ floor(log_2 N) = 33. comb length 34 (0..33). If e+1 > MAXE, then pe*p>n loop wouldn't run. So safe.

Now, recursion performance. Python recursion with many calls may be slow. We can implement dfs recursively but set recursionlimit high. However, the number of calls could be large; recursion overhead significant. Maybe we can add memoization? But memory. Let's estimate calls.

For N=1e10, top loop 9592 primes. For each prime p, call dfs(N//p, i+1). That's 9592 calls. Inside each, loops over primes q ≥ p_{i+1} with q^2≤N//p. The total number of calls is roughly the number of integers m≤N with prime factors nondecreasing? It might be around 1e6. Recursion depth small. 1e6 calls maybe okay in Python if optimized, but combined with two functions maybe 2e6. Could be borderline but acceptable for AtCoder? Need optimize.

We can use memoization for dfs to reduce repeated states? Are states repeated? In the recursion, a state (n, idx) might be reached from different parents? For example, n=N//6 can be reached via p=2 then q=3 (idx after 2) and via p=3 then q=2? But the recursion enforces q≥next prime, so p=3 then q=2 not allowed. Nondecreasing prime factors means each composite has a unique smallest prime factor path. Thus states likely unique. Memoization won't help much and costs memory.

But we can combine D and E in one dfs to halve the number of recursive calls. We'll do that.

Pseudo:

def dfs(n, idx):
    if idx >= len(primes) or primes[idx] > n:
        return (0,0)
    idn = idx_map[n]
    ansD = G_D[idn] - prefD[idx]
    ansE = G_E[idn] - prefE[idx]
    for i in range(idx, len(primes)):
        p = primes[i]
        if p*p > n:
            break
        pe = p
        e = 1
        while pe * p <= n:
            subD, subE = dfs(n // pe, i+1)
            fD_e = comb[e]
            fE_e = fE(p,e)
            ansD += fD_e * subD + comb[e+1]
            ansE += fE_e * subE + fE(p,e+1)
            pe *= p
            e += 1
    return ansD % MOD, ansE % MOD

Need ensure ansD/ansE are modded to avoid huge ints. We can mod at end of each dfs. But ans accumulates over loops; numbers can grow huge (MOD^2 * calls). We should mod periodically. In Python, big ints slow. We can do ansD %= MOD after each addition? That would be many mod operations. Instead, keep ansD and ansE as ints and mod at end; but ansD could be sum of up to maybe thousands terms each ~MOD^2 (1e18), so ansD ~1e21, still fine. Actually subD is modulo MOD (<1e9), fD_e <MOD, product <1e18. Number of terms per dfs maybe up to thousands, sum <1e21, Python big int 70 bits, fine. At end mod. For top-level, ansD sum over all calls could be large but each dfs returns modded, so top ans terms ~1e18 * 9592 ~1e22, fine. So mod at end of each dfs is okay.

But inside dfs, when adding `comb[e+1]` and fE, these are <MOD. Good.

Now, let's think about the correctness of G_E formula with hChi. hChi = ∑_{p≤v} χ(p). χ(p)=1 for p≡1, -1 for p≡2, 0 for p=3. Then h1+hChi = 2*π_1(v). Yes. Add 1 for p=3. So G_E = M*(π_1 + [v≥3]). Good.

Now, one issue: In the DFS, for each prime p_i, we loop e starting at 1 while pe*p <= n. But what about f(p^e) for e where p^e ≤ n but p^{e+1} > n? These are pure powers p^e. Are they counted? Let's verify. For e=1, if p^2≤n, the loop includes +f(p^2) for pure power p^2. What about pure power p^1 (the prime p)? It is counted in G(n)-pref. For e=2, if p^3≤n, loop includes +f(p^3). In general, pure power p^{e+1} is added in iteration e. The largest pure power p^E with p^E≤n but p^{E+1}>n is added in iteration e=E-1 if p^E≤n? Condition for iteration e=E-1: pe=p^{E-1}, pe*p=p^E≤n, so yes. What about p^1? It's in G. What about p^0=1? final add. Thus all pure powers counted. Numbers with multiple prime factors counted via f(p^e)*S(n/pe, i+1), where S includes primes/composites with prime factors ≥p_{i+1}. This counts p^e * m where m>1 and all prime factors ≥p_{i+1}. Since m>1, the total number has at least two prime factors (or more). Good.

Now, let's write the final code.

Pseudo code:

import sys, math
MOD=998244353

def solve():
    N,M=map(int,sys.stdin.readline().split())
    if N==1:
        print(0); return
    sqrtN=math.isqrt(N)
    # sieve primes up to sqrtN
    is_prime=[True]*(sqrtN+1)
    is_prime[0]=is_prime[1]=False
    for i in range(2, math.isqrt(sqrtN)+1):
        if is_prime[i]:
            step=i; start=i*i
            is_prime[start:sqrtN+1:step]=[False]*(((sqrtN-start)//step)+1)
    primes=[i for i in range(2,sqrtN+1) if is_prime[i]]
    # vals decreasing
    vals=[]
    i=1
    while i<=N:
        v=N//i
        vals.append(v)
        i=N//v+1
    # vals is decreasing
    m=len(vals)
    idx_map={v:idx for idx,v in enumerate(vals)}
    h1=[v-1 for v in vals]
    hChi=[(1 if v%3==1 else 0)-1 for v in vals]
    limit=m
    # local vars for speed
    vals_list=vals
    idx_get=idx_map.__getitem__
    for p in primes:
        p2=p*p
        while limit>0 and vals_list[limit-1] < p2:
            limit-=1
        if limit<=0:
            break
        pm_idx=idx_get(p-1)
        h1_pm=h1[pm_idx]
        hChi_pm=hChi[pm_idx]
        if p==3:
            sp=0
        elif p%3==1:
            sp=1
        else:
            sp=-1
        if sp==0:
            for j in range(limit):
                v=vals_list[j]
                h1[j] -= h1[idx_get(v//p)] - h1_pm
        else:
            for j in range(limit):
                v=vals_list[j]
                ptr=idx_get(v//p)
                h1[j] -= h1[ptr] - h1_pm
                hChi[j] -= sp * (hChi[ptr] - hChi_pm)
    # compute G arrays
    G_D=[0]*m
    G_E=[0]*m
    for j,v in enumerate(vals):
        pi=h1[j]
        chi=hChi[j]
        G_D[j]=(M * pi) % MOD
        pi1=(pi + chi)//2
        cnt=pi1 + (1 if v>=3 else 0)
        G_E[j]=(M * cnt) % MOD
    # precompute comb
    maxe=N.bit_length()+1
    comb=[1]*(maxe+1)
    inv=[0]*(maxe+1)
    for e in range(1,maxe+1):
        inv[e]=pow(e, MOD-2, MOD)
        comb[e]=comb[e-1]*(M+e-1)%MOD*inv[e]%MOD
    # pref
    prefD=[0]*(len(primes)+1)
    prefE=[0]*(len(primes)+1)
    for idx,p in enumerate(primes):
        prefD[idx+1]=(prefD[idx]+M)%MOD
        addE = M if (p==3 or p%3==1) else 0
        prefE[idx+1]=(prefE[idx]+addE)%MOD
    sys.setrecursionlimit(1000000)
    def fE(p,e):
        if p==3:
            return comb[e]
        if p%3==1:
            if e%3==2:
                return 0
            return comb[e]
        else:
            if e&1:
                return 0
            return comb[e]
    def dfs(n, idx):
        if idx >= len(primes) or primes[idx] > n:
            return (0,0)
        idn=idx_map[n]
        ansD=G_D[idn] - prefD[idx]
        ansE=G_E[idn] - prefE[idx]
        for i in range(idx, len(primes)):
            p=primes[i]
            if p*p > n:
                break
            pe=p
            e=1
            while pe * p <= n:
                subD, subE = dfs(n // pe, i+1)
                fD_e=comb[e]
                fE_e=fE(p,e)
                ansD += fD_e * subD + comb[e+1]
                ansE += fE_e * subE + fE(p,e+1)
                pe *= p
                e += 1
        return (ansD % MOD, ansE % MOD)
    d,e=dfs(N,0)
    D=(d+1)%MOD
    E=(e+1)%MOD
    print((D-E)%MOD)

Need check `maxe=N.bit_length()+1`: For N=1e10, bit_length=34, maxe=35. comb length 36. In DFS, e+1 ≤ floor(log_2 N)+1? Actually while pe*p<=n, e+1≤floor(log_p n)≤floor(log_2 N) ≤ bit_length-1? For N=1e10, floor(log2)=33, bit_length=34, maxe=35 safe.

But in first DP, for p=3, sp=0, we skip hChi update. However, for h1, we still need h1_pm = h1[pm_idx] where pm_idx=idx[2]. For N=1e10, idx[2] exists. Good.

Potential bug: In the first DP, for p=3, sp=0, but hChi_pm is not used. Good.

Potential bug: The first DP update for h1 uses `h1[ptr]` where ptr=idx_get(v//p). But `h1[ptr]` might have been updated earlier in the same p if ptr <= j? We argued ptr > j because vals decreasing and v//p < v. Let's prove: vals[j]=v. Since v//p < v (p≥2), and vals is strictly decreasing, the index of v//p is greater than the index of v, which is j. Thus ptr > j. So h1[ptr] not yet updated for current p. Good.

Potential bug: The first DP for hChi with sp=-1 and hChi values negative: `hChi[j] -= sp * (hChi[ptr] - hChi_pm)`. If sp=-1, this adds (hChi[ptr]-hChi_pm). Correct.

Now, let's consider if f_E(p,e) for p=3 and e%? p=3 all allowed. Our fE returns comb[e] for p==3. Good.

Now, possible issue: The first DP for hChi uses χ(p) for p=3 as 0, but in the update for h1, p=3 is processed. For hChi, skipping p=3 means hChi values after DP still include composites with smallest prime factor 3? But their χ value is 0, so sum unaffected. However, for future primes p>3, the update uses hChi[ptr] - hChi[pm_idx]. hChi[ptr] may include numbers divisible by3 with χ=0; subtracting them doesn't affect because their contribution is 0. If we didn't remove numbers divisible by3, hChi includes them with χ=0, so the sum is the same as if they were removed. For future updates, when we subtract composites with smallest prime factor p, we use hChi[ptr] which includes numbers divisible by3 with χ=0; but those numbers m in the composite p*m may be divisible by3, and χ(p*m)=χ(p)*χ(m). If χ(m)=0, the composite contributes 0, so including/excluding doesn't matter. Thus skipping p=3 is safe. Good.

But before final, let's see if there is a simpler way to compute D_M(N) and E_M(N) using Min_25 but we must ensure the DFS doesn't time out. Maybe we can add memoization for dfs to reduce repeated calls? Let's investigate if states can repeat. Consider n=floor(N/a) and idx. The recursion path corresponds to a sorted list of prime factors of a. For a given n, a is not uniquely determined because floor(N/a) same for a range. Different a with same floor could have different largest prime factor, so idx differs. Could the same (n, idx) arise from two different a? Suppose a1 and a2 both yield same n and same next prime index idx. The next prime index idx means the largest prime factor used so far is primes[idx-1]. Thus a1 and a2 have the same largest prime factor? Not necessarily, because the path's last chosen prime is primes[i] where i=idx-1. The previous primes are ≤ that. The product a = p_last * previous. If idx same, p_last same. The previous product a_prev = a / p_last. The n = floor(N/(a_prev * p_last)). Different a_prev could yield same n? Possibly if floor(N/(a_prev*p_last)) same. But the previous state would have been (n*p_last? actually n_prev = floor(N/a_prev)) and idx_prev. The recursion path to this state is unique if the previous state is unique. By induction, states are unique. So memoization not needed.

But the DFS without memoization may still call the same state from different parents if the same n and idx can be reached via different previous primes? idx fixes previous prime, so no.

Now, maybe we can prune the DFS for E when f_E(p^e)=0? No, higher e may be allowed. But if p≡2 and e=1, fE=0, but we still need to recurse for e=2? In the loop, for e=1, we call dfs(n//p, i+1) even if fE_e=0. This call is unnecessary for E but needed for D. Since we combine D and E, we still need subD. For E, if fE_e=0, the term fE_e*subE is 0, but the recursive call still computes both D and E. Could we avoid computing E in subcall if fE_e=0? But the same subcall is needed for D, and it also computes E for other paths? The subcall's E result might be needed for other fE_e? If fE_e=0, we don't need subE for this term, but the subcall is shared with D; computing E inside it adds overhead. We could have separate dfsD and dfsE to skip E when not needed, but that would double recursion overhead? Actually separate dfsD only would be faster for D, and dfsE only for E. Combined computes E in all subcalls even if the parent's fE_e=0, but the subcall's E result might be needed if a different parent with fE_e nonzero reaches the same state? States unique, so if fE_e=0 for this path, the subcall's E result is never used for E? Wait the subcall is reached only from this parent (unique), and if fE_e=0, its E result is multiplied by 0, so not needed. However, the subcall's E result might be needed for the `+fE(p,e+1)`? No, that's separate. So for E, we could avoid recursing when fE_e=0? But the subcall also computes D, and we need D. If we have separate dfsD and dfsE, dfsD would still recurse. Combined computes E in that subcall unnecessarily. This could be significant because for p≡2, fE_e=0 for odd e, many subcalls. But the subcall's E computation is interleaved with D; the overhead is the additional arithmetic and G_E/prefE. Maybe not huge.

Could we implement two separate dfs functions: dfsD and dfsE, and in dfsE skip recursion when fE_e=0? But dfsE still needs to enumerate composites for E; if fE_e=0, the term fE_e*subE=0, but there is also +fE(p^{e+1}) which may be nonzero (e.g., p≡2, e=1, fE(p^2)=nonzero). Do we need to recurse for e=1 to count numbers p * m where m has prime factors ≥next? For E, fE(p)=0, so numbers with exactly one factor p (and other primes) are good? Wait E counts non-good products. If p≡2 and exponent total odd, product is good, so not counted. If p appears with exponent 1 and other primes, total exponent for p is odd, so product good, not non-good. Thus any number whose p-exponent is odd should not be counted in E. Therefore fE(p^e)=0 for odd e, and the term fE(p^e)*subE counts numbers with exactly e factors of p (e odd) times m; these are good, so should not be counted. We can skip the recursive call for E when fE_e=0. But the recursive call also would count numbers with additional p factors? Wait subE counts m with prime factors ≥next, so m may contain p? No, next prime >p, so m has no p. Thus total p-exponent is exactly e. If e odd, product good, so not counted. So skipping subE is correct. For e=2 (even), fE_e nonzero, need subE. For p≡2, e=1 skip, e=2 recurse, e=3 skip, etc. For p≡1, fE_e=0 when e≡2 mod3; skip those. This can reduce E recursion. But combined dfs computes subE for all e because subD needs it. Could we pass a flag to compute only D when fE_e=0? That would complicate but could speed.

Maybe implement dfsD and dfsE separately:
- dfsD(n, idx) computes D only. It recurses for all e.
- dfsE(n, idx) computes E only. It recurses only when fE_e != 0? But wait, even if fE_e=0, the pure power fE(p^{e+1}) might be nonzero and is added without recursion. So no need subE. Thus dfsE can skip recursion when fE_e=0. This reduces E calls significantly.
But then we run two separate recursions, doubling the prime loop overhead. However, dfsD and dfsE each have fewer operations? dfsD still needs all calls. dfsE skips many calls. Combined dfs does one recursion with both, but computes E in all D calls. Which is faster? Hard to say. Combined avoids double loop over primes for D and E, but computes E in subcalls where fE_e=0. The number of D calls is the total states. The number of E calls if skipping zero fE_e might be smaller. But the loop over primes in each state is the main cost; combined does it once per state, separate does it twice for states needed by both. Since E states are subset of D states, separate would do D loop for all states and E loop for subset; total loops > combined. So combined likely better.

We can optimize combined by checking if fE_e==0, we still need subD but not subE. But dfs returns both; we can't avoid computing subE inside the subcall unless we have a mode. We could define dfs(n, idx, needE) where needE indicates whether E result is needed. When fE_e==0, call dfs(n//pe, i+1, needE=False) to compute only D. But the subcall's E might be needed for other terms? Since states unique, if this subcall is only reached from this parent and needE=False, we can skip E computation entirely for that subtree. This could significantly speed E. However, implementing needE flag with recursion returning (D, E or 0) is possible:
def dfs(n, idx, needE):
    if base: return (0,0)
    compute ansD. If needE: compute ansE with G_E/prefE; else ansE=0.
    loop:
        subD, subE = dfs(n//pe, i+1, needE and fE_e != 0? Wait if fE_e==0, this subcall's E is not needed for this term. But could the same subcall be needed for E from a different term? States unique, so no. So pass needE_sub = needE and (fE_e != 0). However, if needE is False, we don't need E anywhere in this subtree, so pass False.
        ansD += fD_e*subD + comb[e+1]
        if needE:
            ansE += fE_e*subE + fE(p,e+1)
    return ansD%MOD, (ansE%MOD if needE else 0)
But careful: Even if fE_e==0, the pure power fE(p^{e+1}) might be nonzero and is added without subE. So needE for the subcall is only for the multiplication term. The subcall's E result is not needed if fE_e==0. So pass needE_sub = needE and (fE_e != 0). This means for p≡2 e=1, subcall computes only D, saving E work. For p≡1 e=2, fE_e=0, save. This could reduce E computations substantially.

Define dfs(n, idx, needE):
    if idx >= len(primes) or primes[idx] > n:
        return (0,0)
    idn=idx_map[n]
    ansD = G_D[idn] - prefD[idx]
    ansE = G_E[idn] - prefE[idx] if needE else 0
    for i in range(idx, len(primes)):
        p=primes[i]
        if p*p > n: break
        pe=p
        e=1
        while pe*p <= n:
            fE_e = fE(p,e) if needE else 0  # if not needE, no need
            sub_needE = needE and (fE_e != 0)
            subD, subE = dfs(n//pe, i+1, sub_needE)
            ansD += comb[e]*subD + comb[e+1]
            if needE:
                ansE += fE_e*subE + fE(p,e+1)
            pe *= p; e +=1
    return ansD%MOD, (ansE%MOD if needE else 0)

But if needE=False, fE_e=0, sub_needE=False, subE=0. ansE remains0. Good.

Let's write dfs with local bindings for speed:
primes_local = primes
prefD_local = prefD
prefE_local = prefE
G_D_local = G_D
G_E_local = G_E
comb_local = comb
MOD_local = MOD
sqrtN_local = sqrtN
len_primes = len(primes)

def dfs(n, idx, needE):
    if idx < len_primes:
        if primes_local[idx] > n:
            return (0,0)
    else:
        if n <= sqrtN_local:
            return (0,0)
        idn = idx_get(n)
        ansD = G_D_local[idn] - prefD_local[len_primes]
        if needE:
            ansE = G_E_local[idn] - prefE_local[len_primes]
        else:
            ansE = 0
        return (ansD % MOD_local, ansE % MOD_local)
    idn = idx_get(n)
    ansD = G_D_local[idn] - prefD_local[idx]
    ansE = G_E_local[idn] - prefE_local[idx] if needE else 0
    for i in range(idx, len_primes):
        p = primes_local[i]
        if p*p > n:
            break
        pe = p
        e = 1
        while pe * p <= n:
            if needE:
                if p == 3:
                    fE_e = comb_local[e]
                    fE_next = comb_local[e+1]
                else:
                    r = p % 3
                    if r == 1:
                        fE_e = comb_local[e] if e % 3 != 2 else 0
                        fE_next = comb_local[e+1] if (e+1) % 3 != 2 else 0
                    else:
                        fE_e = comb_local[e] if (e & 1) == 0 else 0
                        fE_next = comb_local[e+1] if ((e+1) & 1) == 0 else 0
                sub_needE = (fE_e != 0)
            else:
                fE_e = 0
                fE_next = 0
                sub_needE = False
            subD, subE = dfs(n // pe, i+1, sub_needE)
            ansD += comb_local[e] * subD + comb_local[e+1]
            if needE:
                ansE += fE_e * subE + fE_next
            pe *= p
            e += 1
    return (ansD % MOD_local, ansE % MOD_local)

Note: If needE=True and fE_e=0, sub_needE=False, subE=0. If needE=True and fE_e !=0, sub_needE=True. If needE=False, sub_needE=False. Good.

But wait, if needE=True and fE_e=0, we still need to compute fE_next for the pure power. We do. Good.

Now, if needE=False, we don't compute fE_e/fE_next; ansE remains0. Good.

Now, one subtlety: In the normal case, if needE=True, ansE = G_E[idn] - prefE[idx]. This counts allowed primes ≥p_idx. But if idx>=len? handled. If needE=False, ansE=0. Good.

Now, in the base idx>=len and needE=True, ansE = G_E[idn] - prefE[len]. This counts allowed primes >sqrtN. If needE=False, ansE=0. Good.

Now, let's think about the case idx<len but primes[idx]<=n and needE=True, but some of the first idx primes are >n? We argued no. Good.

Now, the final call:
d, e = dfs(N, 0, True)
D = (d + 1) % MOD
E = (e + 1) % MOD
print((D - E) % MOD)

For M=2, sequences product≤2: (1,1) product1 non-good, (1,2),(2,1) product2 good =>2. M=2, ans2. Our formula D=τ_2(1)+τ_2(2)=1+2=3, E=1, ans2. Good.

Now, let's think about if M=0? Constraints M≥1.

Now, one more potential issue: The first DP for hChi uses χ(p) for p=2 as -1. For p=2, sp=-1, hChi[j] += hChi[ptr] - hChi_pm. We wrote:
hChi[j] -= sp * (hChi[ptr] - hChi_pm)
With sp=-1, this is hChi[j] += hChi[ptr] - hChi_pm. In code we can implement branches:
if sp == 1:
    hChi[j] -= hChi[ptr] - hChi_pm
else: # sp == -1
    hChi[j] += hChi[ptr] - hChi_pm
Good.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
import math

MOD = 998244353


def solve():
    N, M = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())

    if N == 1:
        print(0)
        return

    sqrtN = math.isqrt(N)

    # primes up to sqrt(N)
    is_prime = [True] * (sqrtN + 1)
    if sqrtN >= 0:
        is_prime[0] = False
    if sqrtN >= 1:
        is_prime[1] = False
    for i in range(2, math.isqrt(sqrtN) + 1):
        if is_prime[i]:
            start = i * i
            step = i
            is_prime[start:sqrtN + 1:step] = [False] * (((sqrtN - start) // step) + 1)
    primes = [i for i in range(2, sqrtN + 1) if is_prime[i]]
    len_primes = len(primes)

    # distinct values floor(N / i), decreasing
    vals = []
    i = 1
    while i <= N:
        v = N // i
        vals.append(v)
        i = N // v + 1

    m = len(vals)
    idx_map = {v: idx for idx, v in enumerate(vals)}
    idx_get = idx_map.__getitem__

    # Min_25 sieve part 1:
    # h1[v]  -> sum_{p<=v} 1
    # hChi[v]-> sum_{p<=v} chi(p), chi is nontrivial Dirichlet character mod 3
    h1 = [v - 1 for v in vals]
    hChi = [(1 if v % 3 == 1 else 0) - 1 for v in vals]

    limit = m
    for p in primes:
        p2 = p * p
        while limit > 0 and vals[limit - 1] < p2:
            limit -= 1
        if limit <= 0:
            break

        pm_idx = idx_get(p - 1)
        h1_pm = h1[pm_idx]

        if p == 3:
            # chi(3)=0, so hChi is unchanged by removing multiples of 3
            for j in range(limit):
                v = vals[j]
                h1[j] -= h1[idx_get(v // p)] - h1_pm
        else:
            sp = 1 if p % 3 == 1 else -1
            hChi_pm = hChi[pm_idx]
            if sp == 1:
                for j in range(limit):
                    v = vals[j]
                    ptr = idx_get(v // p)
                    h1[j] -= h1[ptr] - h1_pm
                    hChi[j] -= hChi[ptr] - hChi_pm
            else:  # sp == -1
                for j in range(limit):
                    v = vals[j]
                    ptr = idx_get(v // p)
                    h1[j] -= h1[ptr] - h1_pm
                    hChi[j] += hChi[ptr] - hChi_pm

    # G_D[v] = sum_{p<=v} f_D(p), G_E[v] = sum_{p<=v} f_E(p)
    G_D = [0] * m
    G_E = [0] * m
    for j, v in enumerate(vals):
        pi = h1[j]
        chi = hChi[j]
        pi1 = (pi + chi) // 2
        cntE = pi1 + (1 if v >= 3 else 0)
        G_D[j] = (M * pi) % MOD
        G_E[j] = (M * cntE) % MOD

    # binomial C(M+e-1, e) for prime power exponents
    maxe = N.bit_length() + 2
    comb = [1] * (maxe + 1)
    for e in range(1, maxe + 1):
        comb[e] = comb[e - 1] * (M + e - 1) % MOD * pow(e, MOD - 2, MOD) % MOD

    # prefix sums of f(p) over small primes
    prefD = [0] * (len_primes + 1)
    prefE = [0] * (len_primes + 1)
    for idx, p in enumerate(primes):
        prefD[idx + 1] = (prefD[idx] + M) % MOD
        addE = M if (p == 3 or p % 3 == 1) else 0
        prefE[idx + 1] = (prefE[idx] + addE) % MOD

    sys.setrecursionlimit(1 << 20)

    def dfs(n, idx, needE):
        # returns (sum over n>1 with all prime factors >= primes[idx]) for D and E
        if idx < len_primes:
            if primes[idx] > n:
                return (0, 0)
        else:
            if n <= sqrtN:
                return (0, 0)
            idn = idx_get(n)
            ansD = G_D[idn] - prefD[len_primes]
            ansE = (G_E[idn] - prefE[len_primes]) if needE else 0
            return (ansD % MOD, ansE % MOD)

        idn = idx_get(n)
        ansD = G_D[idn] - prefD[idx]
        ansE = (G_E[idn] - prefE[idx]) if needE else 0

        for i in range(idx, len_primes):
            p = primes[i]
            if p * p > n:
                break

            pe = p
            e = 1
            while pe * p <= n:
                if needE:
                    if p == 3:
                        fE_e = comb[e]
                        fE_next = comb[e + 1]
                    else:
                        r = p % 3
                        if r == 1:
                            fE_e = comb[e] if e % 3 != 2 else 0
                            fE_next = comb[e + 1] if (e + 1) % 3 != 2 else 0
                        else:
                            fE_e = comb[e] if (e & 1) == 0 else 0
                            fE_next = comb[e + 1] if ((e + 1) & 1) == 0 else 0
                    sub_needE = (fE_e != 0)
                else:
                    fE_e = 0
                    fE_next = 0
                    sub_needE = False

                subD, subE = dfs(n // pe, i + 1, sub_needE)

                ansD += comb[e] * subD + comb[e + 1]
                if needE:
                    ansE += fE_e * subE + fE_next

                pe *= p
                e += 1

        return (ansD % MOD, ansE % MOD)

    d, e = dfs(N, 0, True)

    totalD = (d + 1) % MOD   # include n=1
    totalE = (e + 1) % MOD   # include n=1
    ans = (totalD - totalE) % MOD
    print(ans)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
